A.2       Properties of the 3j symbols
The Wigner 3j symbol,
                                                                   !
                                                   `1 `2 `3
                                                                       ,                            (A.25)
                                                   m1 m2 m3

encodes the geometrical properties of a system of three vectors that form a triangle, `1 +
`2 + `3 = 0; the elements of the first line, (`1 , `2 , `3 ), must be positive and represent the
magnitudes of the three vectors, while those of the second line, (m1 , m2 , m3 ), must satisfy
−`i ≤ mi ≤ `i and represent the projections of the three vectors on the zenith. The
3j symbol is different from zero only for the configurations that respect the triangular
inequality,

                                           |`i − `j | ≤ `k ≤ `i + `j                                (A.26)

and for those whereby `1z + `2z + `3z = 0, that is

                                             m1 + m2 + m3 = 0 .                                     (A.27)

   The 3j symbol is related to the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients, which are often used in
quantum mechanics to describe the coupling of two angular momentum states, by the

                                                           265
following relation
                                              !
                                `1 `2 `3              (−1)`1 −`2 −m3
                                                  =     √            h `1 m1 `2 m2 |`3 m3 i .                       (A.28)
                                m1 m2 m3                  2 `3 + 1


A.2.1      Symmetries of the 3j symbols
The 3j symbols are symmetric under even permutations of their columns,
                                             !                           !                      !
                                `1 `2 `3               `2 `3 `1                  `3 `1 `2
                                                  =                          =                          ,           (A.29)
                                m1 m2 m3               m2 m3 m1                  m3 m1 m2

and they gain an alternating sign factor after an odd permutation,
                                              !                          !                      !
                                `1 `3 `2                  `2 `1 `3               `3 `2 `1
                                                  =                          =
                                m1 m3 m2                  m2 m1 m3               m3 m2 m1
                                                                                        !
                                                            `1 +`2 +`3   `1 `2 `3
                                                  = (−1)                                    .                       (A.30)
                                                                         m1 m2 m3

Changing the sign of the second line yields a phase factor, too,
                                                      !                                         !
                                 `1  `2  `3                        `1 +`2 +`3    `1 `2 `3
                                                          = (−1)                                    ,               (A.31)
                                −m1 −m2 −m3                                      m1 m2 m3

which implies that
                                                  !
                                    `1 `2 `3
                                                      = 0    if   `1 + `2 + `3    is odd .                          (A.32)
                                    0 0 0

This property will be important in understanding the structure of the intrinsic bispectrum
in Chapter 6.
   As we have anticipated in the previous section, the Gaunt integral can be expressed in
terms of the product of two 3j symbols (see Eq. A.19),
                                    s                                               !                       !
                                        (2`1 + 1)(2`2 + 1)(2`3 + 1) `1 `2 `3            `1 `2 `3
             G `m1 1`2m`32 m3   =                                                                               .   (A.33)
                                                    4π              0 0 0               m1 m2 m3

The Gaunt coefficients possess more symmetries than the 3j symbols; in particular,

   • they are symmetric with respect to any permutation of their columns;

   • they vanish for `1 + `2 + `3 odd, and

   • they are invariant under sign flip of the m, that is G `m11`2m`32 m3 = G `−m
                                                                                1 `2 `3
                                                                                    1 −m2 −m3
                                                                                              .

                                                             266
A.2.2     Orthogonality of the 3j symbols

The 3j symbols are orthogonal with respect to the summation over one column,
                                                     !                   !
                 X                    `1 `2 `3             `1 `2 `3
                        (2 `3 + 1)                                           = δm1 M1 δm2 M2 ,                (A.34)
                `3 m3
                                      m1 m2 m3             M1 M2 m3

and with respect to the summation over two azimuthal numbers,
                                                       !                  !
                              X        `1 `2 `3            `1 `2 L3
                (2 `3 + 1)                                                     = δ`3 L3 δm3 M3 .              (A.35)
                             m1 m2     m1 m2 m3            m1 m2 M3

The last identity implies also that
                                                                   !2
                                           X      `1 `2 `3
                                                                        = 1,                                  (A.36)
                                      m1 m2 m3    m1 m2 m3

a result that will be useful in defining the angle-averaged bispectrum.




A.3      Projecting tensors

To project the Einstein equation to spherical space, we need a prescription to extract the
(`, m) multipoles out of a tensor. In this section we show how to do so by employing a set
of projection vectors, ξ, and matrices, χ.

                                                    ∞

The first 3j symbol also enforces that 1 + `1 + ` is even, thus excluding the contribution with

                                 n                                                                                   o
      L100,0 [ni Vi f (n)] ⊃         f99,−1 V[1] , f99,0 V[0] , f99,1 V[−1] , f101,−1 V[1] , f101,0 V[0] , f101,1 V[−1] .

    In any gauge, the free-streaming term of the linearised Boltzmann equation is given by
ni ki f (n, k) .
               Since we choose to align the zenith with the k vector, the latter only has a
scalar part, k[m] = δm0 k (see Eq. A.43). Thus, the sum over m2 in Eq. A.60 reduces to only
one term:
                                                      ∞         `1
                                                                                                     !         !

                                                            ∞
                                                                                              !        !
                   i                      m
                                                            X
                                                                          `−`1         1 `1 `   1 `1 `

This is a manifestation of the decomposition theorem: when the zenith is aligned with k,
all the sums over the different azimuthal modes collapse and there is no coupling between
the modes. As a result, the only contribution to the (`, m)-th multipole of ni ki f comes
from the multipoles of f with azimuthal mode m. On the other hand, the different angular
scales ` still couple, in analogy with the mode coupling of the Fourier modes that we have
explored in Sec. 3.5.2. For example, the (100, 0) multipole of ni ki f (n) picks up only two

                                                                272
contributions:
                                                       n                          o
                            L100,0 [ni ki f (n)] ⊃         f99,0 k[0] , f101,0 k[0] .

     The Boltzmann equation at second order also contains the terms ni k1i f (n) and ni k2i f (n) .
Having aligned the zenith with k, the wavemodes k1 and k2 are arbitrary vectors for which
k1 [±1] and k2 [±1] do not need not vanish. Therefore, the sum over m0 in Eq. A.60 also in-
cludes the azimuthal modes of f with m1 = m ± 1, meaning that the decomposition theorem
does not apply for the quadratic part of the second-order equations.

                                        Geometry of the wavemodes
                                                                                        B
The non-linear transfer functions are defined inside a convolution integral over two dummy
wavemodes, k1 and k2 :

                               (1)
                X`m (k) = T`m (k) Φ(k)                                                  (B.1)

                             dk1 dk2
                         Z
                                                      (2)
                     +               δ(k1 + k2 − k) T`m (k1 , k2 , k) Φ(k1 ) Φ(k2 ) ,
                              (2π)3

where the (`, m) indices come from the decomposition in spherical harmonics of the direc-
                                                                     (2)
tional dependence of X, as explained in Appendix A. In principle, T`m depends on the 9
coordinates of the wavemodes: the magnitudes k1 , k2 and k ; the polar angles θ1 , θ2 and θ ;
the azimuthal angles φ1 , φ2 and φ . In solving the Boltzmann-Einstein differential system
      (2)
for T`m , however, we adopt the following simplifying assumptions that reduce the number
of independent parameters to 3, which we choose to be the three magnitudes; we shall
                                             (2)
denote the resulting transfer function as Te`m (k1 , k2 , k) .
   First, we solve the system only for those configurations where the polar axis is aligned
with k . That is, we always take

                                            θ = φ = 0,                                  (B.2)

which also implies kx = ky = k[±1] = 0 . The statistical isotropy of the Universe ensures that
 (2)
T`m can be obtained in the other configurations by performing a rotation of the polar axis,

                                                 276
as we will detail in Sec. 6.2.1 where we compute the intrinsic bispectrum.
   Secondly, we note that the Dirac delta function enforces k = k1 + k2 . This allows to
express θ1 , θ2 and φ2 as functions of the other variables,

                   k 2 + k12 − k22                              k 2 − k12 + k22
       cos θ1 =                    ,                cos θ2 =                    ,          φ 2 = φ1 + π ,       (B.3)
                        2 k k1                                       2 k k2

so that only 6 independent parameters are left. Together with the alignment of the polar
axis, the Dirac delta condition allows us to set k1x = −k2x , which implies

                                                k1 sin θ1 = k2 sin θ2 ,                                         (B.4)

an expression that will be useful in Sec. 6.2.2 to optimise the bispectrum computation.
   Finally, we only compute the transfer functions in φ1 = 0 and φ2 = π , so that the k1
and k2 wavevectors both lie in the zx plane. Again, thanks to the statistical isotropy, the
           (2)
value of T`m in the general case is obtained with the simple rotation

                                  (2)                                   (2)
                                Te`m (k1 , k2 , k, φ1 ) = e imφ1 Te`m (k1 , k2 , k, 0) .                        (B.5)

We shall use this property in Eq. 6.23 to analytically solve the φ1 dependence in the
bispectrum integral. To sum up, the second-order transfer function computed by SONG,
   (2)
Te`m (k1 , k2 , k3 ) , is related to the general one by

     (2)                  (2)                                                                               
   Te`m (k1 , k2 , k) = T`m k1 , θ1 (k1 , k2 , k), φ1 = 0, k2 , θ2 (k1 , k2 , k), φ2 = π, k, θ = 0, φ = 0 .     (B.6)



B.1        Rotation
The second-order equations are sourced by terms quadratic in the linear transfer functions;
because the Fourier transform of a product in real space is a convolution in Fourier space
(Eq. 3.65), these quadratic sources are evaluated in the dummy wavemodes k1 and k2 .
                                                                      (2)
For example, the equation for the photon dipole transfer function, T1m , at second order
includes the term

                                              (2)         (1)          (1)
                                          Ṫ1m ⊃ 4 T1m (k1 ) ṪΦ (k2 ) .                                        (B.7)

In SONG, we compute the linear transfer functions only in the direction of the polar axis,

                                        (1)              (1)
                                    Te`m (k1 ) = T`m (k1 , θ1 = 0, φ1 = 0) .                                    (B.8)

The Te (1) (k1 ) ’s cannot be inserted directly in the quadratic sources of the second-order
system, like Eq. B.7, which instead involve the transfer functions in the general direction

                                                           277
k2 . Thanks to statistical isotropy, however, the two are related by a Wigner rotation,
                                            s
                                (1)              4π                (1)
                             T`m (k1 ) =             Y`m (k̂1 ) Te` 0 (k1 ) .                  (B.9)
                                                2`+1

Here we have implicitly used the fact that only the scalar mode exists at first order, since
                                                                           (1)
we assume vanishing initial conditions for the vector and tensor modes: Te`m (k1 ) ∝ δm0 .
    All the quadratic sources in SONG are expressed using Eq. B.9, including the baryon
velocity,
                                               r
                                                     4π              (1)
                               vb[m] (k1 ) =            Y1m (k̂1 ) veb[0] (k1 ) .             (B.10)
                                                      3

It should be noted that, having chosen the azimuthal angle of k1 to be φ1 = 0 and φ1 =
φ1 + π , the Y`m function is always real-valued. This is a favorable property because it
is numerically simpler to evolve a system of real-valued differential equations. (See also
Eq. A.37 of [2] and Eq. A.6 of [1].)



B.2      Symmetrisation
The second-order transfer functions are defined inside a convolution integral (Eq. 3.69)
where k1 and k2 are the integration variables. This reflects the structure of the Boltzmann
and Einstein equations, which, in Fourier space, include the same convolution over the
quadratic sources (Sec. 3.5.2). Because k1 and k2 are dummy variables, the quadratic
sources can be arranged to be symmetric with respect to their exchange:

                                      (2)                    (2)
                                 T`m (k1 , k2 , k) = T`m (k2 , k1 , k) .                      (B.11)

     The k1 ↔ k2 symmetry is exploited in SONG to reduce the computation time of the
transfer functions by half. We do so by building quadratic sources that are symmetric with
respect to the exchange of the magnitudes k1 and k2 . Since θ1 and θ2 are determined by
k1 , k2 and k via Eq. B.3, this choice also ensures that the quadratic sources are symmetric
with respect to θ1 ↔ θ2 . The azimuthal angles of the convolution wavemodes, on the other
hand, are independent from the magnitudes and satisfy φ2 = φ1 + π . Then, the identity
  (2)                 (2)
T`m (k1 , k2 , k) = T`m (k2 , k1 , k) implies

                   (2)                         (2)                              (2)
                 Te`m (k2 , k1 , k) = e imπ Te`m (k1 , k2 , k) = (−1)m Te`m (k1 , k2 , k) .   (B.12)

Thus, by symmetrising the quadratic sources with respect to k1 ↔ k2 we only need to evolve
the transfer functions with k2 ≥ k1 ; the other configurations are obtained by multiplication
with the (−1)m factor. We shall use this fact in Sec. 6.2.2 to perform the bispectrum

                                                      278
integral.
